L4: a rate-pending row displays its original amount and currency, and is visibly distinct from a converted one. **Vacuous traps**: rewriting snapshotted entries, which breaks hard rule 3 and silently restates history; converting at today's rate; asserting the pending COUNT fell without asserting a specific entry's money; showing the original amount without marking it unconverted, so a USD number reads as home currency |
| **[ ]** RV.139 | **The client never asks for exchange rates at all - and Low Power Mode is NOT the reason.** **STILL OPEN after the 2026-09-08 run - the symptom is not fixed, two causes are now ruled OUT.** The single-flight latch I hypothesised does **not** exist: `await task.value` on a `Task<Void, Never>` never throws and always resumes once the child finishes, and the runtime retains the creator continuation, so a cancelled or abandoned creator still reaches the clear. The agent probed it directly (a cancelled creator resumes, `cleared=true`) and reproduced nothing, then reported the negative instead of forcing a test to match the guess. What shipped is hardening (the fetch task now owns the clear, so the slot is structurally unable to latch) and the **`rates.refresh` event with `outcome: attempted|joined|deferred`** - which is what will actually name the branch from a production log. **Next step is a log, not a code change**: get one session from a device on a build carrying that event and read which outcome appears, or whether the line is absent entirely (which would mean `refresh()` is never called, moving the search to `runAutomaticPass`). Product owner, 2026-09-08: *"there are no requests to get exchange rates from the client."* Confirmed across every log supplied: builds `1.0.0+841`, `+857` and `+864` produce `auth.refresh`, `sync.pull`, `sync.push` and once `GET /v1/config/`, and **not one `/v1/rates/pack`**. Public unauthenticated GETs ARE logged - the `GET /v1/config/ -> 499` line on 2026-09-07 proves it - so a request reaching this origin would appear. **Everything on the client is wired correctly**: `AppRates.store` is built with a real `RemoteRateFetcher` (`ManualFillUpCurrencySupport.swift:51,286-289`), `endpoint()` builds `baseURL/v1/rates/pack` on the **same host** as sync (`RemoteRateFetcher.swift:69-71`), so this is not [RV.68]'s missing-`/v1` shape, and `TabRoots.swift:532` calls `AppRates.refresh()` inside `runAutomaticPass`. **The obvious explanation is ruled out by the logs themselves.** `RateStore.refresh` returns early without any HTTP when `LowPowerPolicy.defers(work:.ratePackRefresh, trigger:.background, lowPowerMode:)` is true - but `PowerState.swift:83-84` defers `.syncCycle` on **exactly the same condition**, and sync is running in every one of these sessions. If Low Power Mode were on, sync would be silent too. It is not. So the pass reaches sync and something between sync and the rate fetch swallows the call | **Find why the awaited chain never produces a request, and prove it with a test that would fail today.** `runAutomaticPass` (`TabRoots.swift:513-540`) awaits in order: config, monthly summary, `sync.runOpportunisticSync()`, `AppRates.refresh()`, `inbox.drainOutbox()`. Candidates to rule out **with evidence, not reasoning**: the single-flight `inFlightRefresh` task in `RateStore.refresh` (`:162-176`) never clearing, so every later call joins a completed-or-dead task and returns without fetching; an earlier `await` in the pass never returning on device; and whether `runAutomaticPass` is reached at all on a warm foreground versus only at cold launch (`didRunAutomaticPass` gates it, `:332`). **Instrument before fixing**: `docs/LOGGING.md` allows shape, so a log line recording that a pack fetch was attempted, deferred, or joined an in-flight task would have answered this in one session and is worth having regardless of the cause | L1: a second `refresh()` after the first completes issues a **second** request - the single-flight must not become a permanent latch. Assert the transport's request count, not that the call returned. L3: one simulated foreground cycle with Low Power Mode **off** produces exactly one `/rates/pack` request. L1: the deferral path is only taken when the mode is actually on. **Vacuous traps**: asserting `refresh()` returned true, which it does on the join path; testing with a fresh store, where the latch cannot have formed; concluding "Low Power Mode" without checking that sync deferred too - that is the reasoning this row exists to correct |
| **[ ]** RV.138 | **The daily carry-forward has no horizon, and [RV.135] just made that matter.** Found by the RV.135 agent and confirmed: `RatesJobService.CarryForwardAsync` (`:104-125`) orders each quote's published rows by date and walks `for (var day = published[0].Date; day <= today; ...)`, inserting a carried-forward row for **every** gap day. `published[0]` is the **OLDEST** published row, so the walk starts wherever history begins. Until now that was days after first deploy and the cost was invisible. **[RV.135] makes a deep backfill reachable**: the moment a device asks for a 2015 span - which the product owner's own multi-year import will - the oldest `ecb` row becomes 2015, and the next daily pass inserts a row for every gap day from then to today, for every quote. That is roughly **4,000 dates x ~30 quotes** on one pass. The carry itself is stepwise and NOT a 2015 rate smeared forward (the loop advances its `lastRate` as it passes each published row), so this is a **volume and occupancy** problem, not a wrong-number one: the inserted rows then occupy dates that genuine demand would otherwise backfill with a real published value, and [RV.36]'s supersede only fires for a date someone actually asks for | **Bound the walk.** The demand backfill already has `CarryBackWindowDays = 14` and the daily carry-forward has nothing - decide its horizon and write it into `docs/SCHEMA.md` -> Exchange rates beside the existing one. The obvious shape is to start the walk at `today - N` rather than at the oldest row, since carrying a decade of gap days serves no device: a device asking for an old date gets it through the demand path, which fetches the real value. **Do not** solve it by making the demand backfill store less - the deep rows are the point of [RV.135] | L2: a base whose oldest published row is a decade old inserts a **bounded** number of carried rows on one daily pass - assert the count, not that it "completes". L2: a gap day inside the horizon still carries forward exactly as today. L2: a decade-old published row is **not** deleted or altered by the pass. **Vacuous traps**: asserting the job runs; testing with a fresh database whose oldest row is days old, where the defect cannot appear; bounding the insert count by batching rather than by a horizon, which keeps the same rows arriving over more passes |

L2: one pass over 50 historical dates makes a bounded number of upstream requests, asserted as a number. L1: a date the history genuinely lacks (a weekend) resolves by the documented carry rule or stays absent, never guessed. **Vacuous traps**: asserting the request was enqueued rather than that a rate came back; testing with today's date, which already works; re-downloading the full history per date and calling it fixed; removing the `fileDate != date` guard to make dates resolve |

L4 `FlaggedEntriesUITests`: accepting from the row drops the count, and the count reaches zero when the last one is accepted. **Vacuous traps**: hiding the row in `FlaggedEntriesView` without touching the validator - the flag returns on the next sync, which is the defect; asserting the flag is gone in memory without re-running validation; testing acceptance on a freshly created entry rather than an IMPORTED one with a real gap, which is the case the row is about; a bulk-accept that makes every assertion pass and every future warning meaningless |

Sometimes, even a city."* Tankbook already has the two halves this needs: **tire sets** with swap history (P3.3) and a **reminder + notification** pipeline. **The country is now derivable** without asking - [RV.115]'s chain is receipt in hand, then the user's own history, then device region, then `detectedCountry` | **The obvious shape is FORBIDDEN, and the doc says so - read this before designing.** `docs/NOTIFICATIONS.md:11` states it as a hard rule: **"No user-visible remote push exists at all in v1, and no marketing push will ever exist"** - the only remote push is the content-free silent sync nudge. A server that decides "this user needs winter tyres" and pushes it would also have to know where the user is and what tyres they own, which is hard rule 9 (the server never reads domain meaning) broken on top. **So the architecture inverts, and it is the one this repo already uses twice**: a **backend job** polls the forecast provider (the API key stays server-side, `docs/SECURITY.md`) and publishes a small **seasonal-advisory pack** as public, ETag'd **reference data** - the same contract as `GET /reference/fuel-price-bands` and [RV.115]'s station brands, carrying per-region rows and taking no query parameters. The **device** matches its region, decides whether it applies, and schedules a **local** notification through the existing reminder path. The server never learns who owns winter tyres. **Two inputs, not one**: the weather crossing AND the **statutory window** (Russia and Estonia mandate winter tyres by date; Germany's rule is situational) - a legal deadline is not a forecast and must not be presented as one. **A schema gap blocks the useful half**: `TireSet` is `{ id, vehicleId, name, purchaseExpenseId }` (`Entities.swift:406-414`) - **no season and no 'currently fitted'** - so today the app cannot say "swap from summer to winter", only "it is the season". Decide whether to add them or to keep the advisory generic, and say which. **Quiet, twice a year, opt-out** - [RV.121] is the standing lesson about a card that shouts; a seasonal nudge that fires more than twice a year is a bug. **Degrade to silence**: no pack, no network, no location - nothing happens and nothing errors (hard rule 1) | L1: the crossing rule fires once per direction per season for a fixture forecast, and NOT on a single warm day inside winter. L1: a statutory window and a weather crossing produce different messages, and the statutory one names the date. L1: no region match yields silence, not a default region. L2: the pack is public, ETag'd and carries no user identifier. L4: the notification is local, dismissible, and switchable off in Settings. **Vacuous traps**: sending it as a remote push, which the hard rule above forbids; letting the server decide per user, which needs their location and their tyres; asserting the pack downloads rather than that the device decided correctly from it; firing on every cold day instead of on the seasonal crossing; testing only the summer-to-winter direction |
| **[x]** RV.121 | **The consumption anomaly card guesses causes it cannot know, and says them out loud.** Product owner, 2026-09-07: *"don't think that calculating and showing anomalies on average consumption means something (besides budget) ... I can drive 1000 km a day, I can stay at a point with the engine running for 40 minutes in the winter - this all affects my consumption. And we can't say anything about it as we don't know what has happened."* **The code agrees with the objection**: `AnomalyEngine.minimumRelativeDrift = 0.12` fires at a 12% rise against a year-ago baseline (`AnomalyEngine.swift:194,220-221`), and the card then prints `"Consumption is up X% vs a year ago"` above **`"Likely causes: tire pressure, air filter, winter"`** (`L10n.swift:571-588`). That last line is a **causal claim the app has no evidence for** - it cannot see a motorway week, an idling hour in a Russian winter, a tow, a roof box or a different driver, and naming three plausible causes makes a guess look like a diagnosis. **And the B2C/B2B split matters**: a fleet manager comparing drivers and vehicles genuinely wants variance analysis; a private owner mostly wants to know their BUDGET moved. Tankbook is the single owner's log (`docs/COMPETITORS.md` -> the Drivvo seam), so the fleet reading is not ours to serve in v1 | **Keep the signal, drop the diagnosis, and lower the volume.** Concretely: **delete the 'likely causes' line** - it is the part that cannot be defended; **say what changed and what it costs**, which is the reading a private owner actually has ("this month is running higher than usual - about X more per month at today's prices"); **move to a monthly granularity** rather than a rolling drift that can fire mid-month on a single long trip; and **choose the threshold precisely and write down why** - 12% is currently a constant with no recorded derivation, and the corpus and the owner's own 250-fill history are the evidence available (their spread is 4.973-10.989 L/100km around a 6.827 mean, so a 12% band is well inside normal variation for one car). **It must not scream** (product owner's word): amber is attention and this is barely that - decide whether it is a card at all or a line on the month divider ([RV.119]). **Do not** remove the engine: the rolling/baseline computation is sound and [RV.118]'s coverage line makes it honest; it is the PRESENTATION and the threshold that overclaim | L1: the chosen threshold is derived from data and the derivation is in the doc - assert the constant against the stated rule, not against itself. L1: a month whose rise is inside the band produces no signal. L1: no user-facing string asserts a CAUSE - grep the catalog for the causes phrasing and assert it is gone. L4: the surface is quiet, dismissible and never blocks. **Vacuous traps**: retuning the number without recording why, which is how 0.12 got there; keeping the causes line and softening its wording, which is the same claim in a quieter voice; asserting the card renders rather than that it no longer diagnoses |
| **[ ]** RV.122 **[v1.1]** | **Reminder chips across the top of Home, with 'see all' as the last chip.** Product owner, 2026-09-07, from the Drivvo home screen: a horizontal strip of chips - *Next fill 492 370 km · 06 Sep · in 579 km*, *Insurance · 91 days ago*, *Inspection · 63 days ago* - each saying **what is coming and when or how far**, overdue ones marked, and the owner's addition: **the last chip opens the full reminders list**. Tankbook's Home shows **one** banner - `ReminderBanner.bannerReminder` is a single `.attention` row (`HomeBanners.swift`) - plus a separate cross-car count from [RV.76], so a driver with three things due sees one of them and a number | **A scannable strip that says what and when, and ends with the door.** Each chip carries the reminder's own next step in its own terms - a date reminder says the date and how overdue, a distance reminder says the odometer and **how far away** it is, which is the framing Drivvo gets right and the banner lacks. **The last chip is 'All reminders'** and is a chip like the others, which solves the affordance problem [RV.83] just fixed on the Garage strip: a door that looks like a door. **Reuse the derived data** - `ReminderLifecycle` already derives `.attention` at read time and [RV.76] already computes the cross-car count (hard rule 2, never stored). **Amber is attention and overdue is still amber** (hard rule 5): red lives only in system dialogs, so an overdue chip is amber with words that say overdue, never a red chip. **Decide what an EMPTY strip does** - nothing due is the common case and the strip must vanish rather than say 'nothing', which is the mistake the empty-state rules already warn about | L1: the chip set for a fixture is exactly the due/overdue reminders in due order, and the trailing 'All reminders' chip is always last. L1: a distance reminder's chip shows the remaining distance from the current odometer, a date reminder's shows the date - assert both forms. L4: tapping a chip opens that reminder, tapping the last opens the merged list; with nothing due the strip is absent, not empty. Screenshots EN+RU - RU expands 20-30% and chips are exactly where that bites. **Vacuous traps**: asserting the strip exists rather than which chips and in what order; a red overdue chip (hard rule 5); rendering an empty strip; testing one reminder, where ordering and the trailing chip cannot fail |
| **[ ]** RV.123 **[v2]** | **Transfer a car to another user - the acquisition loop.** Product owner, 2026-09-07: *"interesting and viral feature for 2.x - transfer a car from one to another user. A transfer hands over the car **and its log** - which is exactly the moment the new owner has a reason to install the app, so the feature is a growth loop, not only a convenience | **Design it as a MOVE with an explicit handshake, and decide the five things that make it safe.** (1) **What moves**: the vehicle, its entries, reminders, tire sets and attachments - and what does NOT (the seller's other cars, obviously; their account, their stations?). (2) **What the seller keeps**: a sold car's history is also the seller's own spending record, so decide between a true move, a copy, or a move that leaves the seller a read-only archive - and say why (hard rule 8 says nothing is lost silently, and losing five years of one's own spend on tapping 'transfer' would be exactly that). (3) **The recipient may have NO account** - that is the acquisition moment and the flow must survive it: an invite that outlives the install, and a transfer that cannot be claimed twice. (4) **The server side is a new capability**: moving records between accounts rewrites `account_id` on rows the server still never READS, so hard rule 9 holds in spirit - but it is an account-state change with its own failure modes, and **blobs are per-account with per-account dedupe** ([RV.44]/P4.3), so the attachments must move or be re-keyed rather than dangle. (5) **It must be reversible or confirmed like a deletion** - a mistaken transfer is worse than a mistaken delete, because the data is now in someone else's account and the 30-day undo window cannot reach it | L2: a transfer moves every vehicle-scoped row exactly once, the recipient sees the whole history, and the seller's OTHER cars are untouched. L2: a claim link cannot be used twice, and an unclaimed transfer expires. L2: attachments resolve for the recipient and are not orphaned for the seller. L4: the seller's confirmation states exactly what leaves and what stays. **Vacuous traps**: testing with a car that has no attachments, which hides the blob half; asserting the vehicle moved without asserting the entries and reminders did; a transfer that silently destroys the seller's own spending history; testing only a recipient who already has an account, which skips the acquisition case the feature exists for |
| **[ ]** RV.118 **[v1.1]** | **A derived figure should say what it is made of.** From the Drivvo report screens (product owner, 2026-09-07): beside its consumption average Drivvo prints *"190 of 250 fills were full-tank (76.0%) - the average is counted from one full tank to the next"*, with a progress bar for the ratio. **That one line is the most valuable thing on their reports**, and it is the thing this project already believes: a figure the app derived (hard rule 2) is only as good as the data under it, and the user cannot judge it without knowing the coverage. **Tankbook has exactly this gap**: Home shows "5.2 L/100km" from the rolling-90-day / floor-3 model and never says whether that is three fills or thirty, how many were full-tank, or that the window is 90 days at all. The same is true of the month spend, the price average and the anomaly baseline (`AnomalyEngine.swift:92-101` computes a rolling value against a baseline window and the user sees neither window) | **Every headline number gains a one-line provenance: the window, the sample, and the coverage.** "From 18 fills in the last 90 days; 14 were full-tank" is the shape. **It is not a footnote nobody reads** - it is the difference between a number and a claim, and it is what makes a LOW number trustworthy rather than suspicious. **Reuse what the engine already knows**: the consumption model, the rolling/baseline windows and the fill counts exist; this row surfaces them rather than recomputing (a second computation that can disagree with the first is the defect, see [RV.117]). **Decide what a number with too little behind it says** - the floor-3 rule already means some cars have no average, and "not enough data yet, 2 of 3 fills" is a better answer than a blank or a lie. **Do not** add a percentage bar for its own sake; the number that matters is how much of the user's own data the figure used | L1: the provenance for a known fixture is exact - window, sample size, full-tank count - and changes when a fill is added. L1: a car under the floor reports 'not enough data' with the count it does have, never a computed average. L4: Home and Trends show it, and it survives a language switch (hard rule 10, a full localised phrase - never a concatenation of count + word). **Vacuous traps**: asserting the string exists rather than the NUMBERS in it; computing the coverage separately from the consumption engine so the two can disagree; showing 100% for a car with three fills, which is true and useless - the sample size is the point |
| **[ ]** RV.119 **[v1.1]** | **The Log's month divider could carry the month, not just its spend.** Drivvo's history groups by month and the header reads *"AUGUST 2025 - 15 072 RUB · 585 km · 6.414 L/100km · cost per km 25.76 RUB"*, and the month before it adds *"spending 7% lower than June"* (product owner, 2026-09-07). Tankbook's divider shows the month name and its spend - now typed `.complete/.partial/.pending` by [RV.106] - and nothing else, so the question the app exists to answer ("what did this car cost me") is answered one month at a time with no distance, no efficiency and no sense of direction. Their entry rows also carry the fill's own consumption inline (`6,414 л/100км`), which Tankbook computes and does not show on the row | **Extend the divider, and add the month-over-month delta.** Distance, consumption and cost-per-km are all derived from rows the section already holds (hard rule 2 - never stored). **The comparison is the part with teeth**: "7% lower than June" is what makes the number mean something, and it is also the part that can lie - a partial month, a month with a pending rate ([RV.106]) or a month with one fill must NOT be compared as if complete. Decide what an incomparable month says and say it. **Respect the typed total**: a `.pending` or `.partial` month has no honest spend, so it has no honest delta either. **Per-row consumption** is the smaller half: show what the engine already computes for that fill, and omit it (never `-` or `0`) where the fill cannot yield one - the first fill, a partial tank, a broken timeline | L1: the divider's distance, consumption and cost-per-km match the rows beneath it, to the cent and the tenth. L1: a `.pending`/`.partial` month yields NO delta, and a month with one fill yields none either. L1: the delta's sign and magnitude are right across a year boundary. L4: the divider renders in EN and RU as full localised phrases. **Vacuous traps**: comparing against a partial month, which is the whole trap; asserting a delta exists rather than its value; showing cost-per-km for a month whose distance is unknown; a fixture of one complete month, where no comparison is possible and every assertion passes |
| **[ ]** RV.120 **[v1.1]** | **Fill pattern: how far between fills, how often, how far is left, what the month will cost.** Drivvo's "Режим заправок" card (product owner, 2026-09-07): distance between fills 538 km, frequency 7.269 days, **estimated range left 850 km**, **month forecast 27 448 RUB** - and it sits directly above the full-tank coverage line, which is the honest reason to trust or distrust it. Tankbook derives consumption and spend but answers none of these, and the two that matter to a driver standing at a pump are **how far this tank goes** and **what this month is going to cost** | **Derive all four locally, and gate them on the same coverage [RV.118] surfaces.** Distance between fills and frequency are arithmetic over the log. **Range left needs the tank size and the last fill** - `Vehicle.tankCapacityL` is a catalog pre-fill the user may never have corrected (hard rule 13), so a range built on a wrong tank is confidently wrong: decide what it does when the capacity is unconfirmed, and prefer saying nothing to guessing. **The month forecast is a PREDICTION** and must be labelled as one - the honest form is "on this month's pace", not a figure that looks measured. **Do not** show any of the four for a car under the consumption floor; that is precisely the car whose numbers are noise | L1: distance between fills and frequency match a known fixture, and both are absent for a car with one fill. L1: range left is absent when the tank capacity is unconfirmed, and correct when it is. L1: the forecast equals the stated method on a fixture - assert the arithmetic, not that a number appeared. L4: EN and RU, and every one of the four is omitted rather than zeroed when it cannot be computed. **Vacuous traps**: a forecast that extrapolates from two days of a month and reads as fact; a range figure from an uncorrected catalog tank capacity; testing a car with a full year of dense history, where nothing is ever absent - the absent cases are the design |
| **[ ]** RV.117 **[v1.1]** | **A timeline conflict should show the neighbourhood and the valid RANGE, not just quote one row.** Product owner, 2026-09-07, from a Drivvo screen worth copying (screenshot in the session record). Drivvo, on an odometer that contradicts its date, shows: a **chart of the surrounding entries** with the trend through the valid ones and the offending point plotted off it (489 590 / 490 500 / **490 200** / 491 206 / 491 791 across 14/06-24/08); the two rows that bracket it, the previous and "this entry"; a link to the consumption from the previous one; and - **the part that makes it actionable** - a computed bound: *"on 13/07/2025 the odometer must be between 490 500 and 490 983 km; if 490 200 is correct, the date must be..."*. **It names the valid interval for each field GIVEN the other**, so the user learns which of the two is wrong rather than only that something is. **What Tankbook does today** (`docs/ERRORS.md:146`, F9a): amber, one conflicting entry quoted ("Aug 17 already recorded 119 486 km"), and ranked fixes - fix odometer (preselected), fix date (explicit override), save anyway (flagged). That is honest and it is thin: one neighbour, no range, no picture, and **nothing at all for an entry flagged in the PAST**, which is the case the owner hit after importing years of history | **Show the neighbourhood and compute both bounds.** The chart is the presentation; the **bidirectional interval is the substance** and must be derived, never stored (hard rule 2): given the entries either side, the odometer valid for this date, and the dates valid for this odometer. **Do not auto-correct** - the app suggests and the user decides (hard rule 13), and the corpus is the reason: Vision has misread a digit at confidence 1.00, so a computed 'should be' is a default input, never a fact. **Reuse the engine, do not fork it**: `TimelineValidator` already finds the previous/next pair and produces `ResolutionSuggestion`s (`TimelineValidator.swift:211`), so this row extends what it returns rather than computing a second opinion that can disagree with the flag. **It must work from the FLAGGED LIST, not only at save time** - that is the owner's second point and the reason this is more than cosmetics: an entry flagged during an import is one the user cannot reason about from memory, and the neighbourhood is the only evidence they have. **Coordinate with [RV.104]**: accepting a flag is the honest last resort, and this row is what makes it a last resort rather than the only option. **Palette**: amber is attention (hard rule 5) - the offending point is amber, and red stays inside system dialogs; numbers in DIN with `tabular-nums` (hard rule 6) | L1 (the substance): given a previous and next entry, the computed odometer interval for a date and the date interval for an odometer are correct at the boundaries - assert the endpoints, not that a range exists. L1: an entry with no NEXT entry (the newest) has an open-ended upper bound and says so rather than inventing one. L1: the intervals agree with the flag - a value inside the interval is never flagged, a value outside always is. L4: the chart renders from Edit entry AND from the flagged list, and the offending point is distinguishable by more than colour (hard rule 5). **Vacuous traps**: shipping the chart without the intervals, which is decoration; computing the range separately from `TimelineValidator` so the two can disagree; testing only a middle entry, where both neighbours exist and the open-ended cases never arise; asserting the chart appears rather than what it says; auto-applying the suggested value |
| **[ ]** RV.115 | **There is no station BRAND list, so every spelling of one chain becomes a different station.** Product owner, 2026-09-07, prompted by the Drivvo export: its `Азс` column holds `Газпром`, `Sinooil`, `Royal Petrol`, `Гелиос`, `Green`, `Petrol Asia` - free text, and the same chain appears in the corpus receipts as `Газпромнефть`, `ГАЗПРОМНЕФТЬ` and `Gazpromneft`. **What exists today**: `Station` is a per-account synced entity with `name`, `brand`, `location`, `favorite`, `defaults` (`Entities.swift:445-456`) - and **nothing normalises it**: there is no brand vocabulary, no matcher, and `ImportConversion` creates no `Station` at all, so an imported fill keeps its station as text the app cannot group by. The consequence compounds with every importer: 'what do I spend at Gazprom' cannot be answered when four spellings are four stations, and [PJ.19]'s station ranking (written in JOURNEYS/SCHEMA, never called) has nothing stable to rank | **Design a station BRAND list as reference data, and keep individual forecourts per-account.** The distinction is the whole design: a global list of every petrol station on earth is a maintenance and privacy problem nobody asked for, while a list of ~100 BRANDS with their alias spellings is small, stable, and enough to group by. **Follow `/catalog`'s shape exactly** (`docs/API.md` -> `GET /catalog`): public, no auth, ETag + `Cache-Control`, a versioned pack the client replaces wholesale, `since_version` delta - it is reference data, not a domain query, so hard rule 9 is satisfied and no new exception is needed. **The pack carries aliases per brand**, because the same chain is `Газпром` / `Газпромнефть` / `Gazpromneft` / `G-Drive` depending on who typed it and in which script. **Both tiers**: the backend serves and versions the pack; the client matches a free-text station name to a brand when it creates a `Station` (import, capture, manual entry) and **shows the brand it chose**. **Hard rule 13 governs the match**: a matched brand is a DEFAULT the user can change, and a name the user typed or corrected is theirs permanently - no later pack update may rewrite it (`docs/SYNC.md` -> Reference data already states this rule for the vehicle catalog; state it here too). **Decide and write down** what happens to a name that matches nothing: it stays as the user's own station with no brand, which must be a first-class state and not an error. **Ordering is DECIDED (product owner, 2026-09-07) and it happens on the DEVICE**: the pack carries `country` per brand and the client orders **the receipt in hand (a capture names the station, and its currency, VAT rate and script imply the country - the owner's addition, 2026-09-07), then brands the user has already used, then the device region's, then the rest**. The owner raised it as "order by IP country, fall back to previous entries when a VPN lies" - the goal is right and the placement inverts, because the user's own history beats IP in **every** case rather than only under a VPN, only the device has it (hard rules 1 and 9), geo-IP would force a `Vary` that makes a public ETag'd pack uncacheable, and `GET /reference/fuel-price-bands` already sets this precedent by carrying `country` per row and taking no query parameters. **Plus the cold-start hint the owner proposed (2026-09-07)**: the server may return a coarse `detectedCountry` derived from the connection IP, but **only on a per-request, uncacheable response** (`/auth/session`, `/sync/*`, `/import/parse`, `/extract`) and **never on a cacheable one** (`/catalog`, `/config`, `/rates*`, `/reference/*`) - `/import/parse` matters most because it is the one uncacheable call a signed-out user makes, and importing is when the brand list is needed. It is **computed per request and never stored** (no fifth place holding user content, hard rule 9's list), is a **default input outranked by the user's own history** (hard rule 13), and is **absent-able** - a client that never receives it orders exactly as before. See `docs/API.md` | L1: four spellings of one chain match one brand, and a name matching nothing yields a station with a nil brand rather than a failure. L1: a user-edited station name survives a pack update (hard rule 13, the same test shape the catalog has). L2: the endpoint is public, ETag'd, and a `since_version` delta replaces the held set. L4: an imported fill shows the brand the matcher chose, and the user can change it. **Vacuous traps**: shipping a brand list with no matcher, which normalises nothing; matching by exact string, which is what the app effectively does today; asserting the pack downloads rather than that two spellings group together; a matcher so eager it merges two genuinely different local chains - name the false-positive guard |
| **[ ]** RV.116 | **An import silently drops the columns it does not support, and never says which.** Product owner, 2026-09-07: *"worth to say for this program, what data we don't support (as a notification), such as driver and other fields"*. **Measured on the Drivvo export**: of 29 refuelling columns, Tankbook has no home for `Водитель` (driver - the fleet feature that is Drivvo's whole positioning), `Метод оплаты` (payment method), `Тип расхода`, `Скидка` (discount), the second- and third-fuel blocks, and the EV columns (charge type, start/end %, duration) when the row is a liquid fill; the `##Service` and `##Expense` sections carry `Заголовок` and `Название сервиса` with no destination either. MFM has the same shape with different names. **Today the parser reads what it understands and the rest evaporates** - the user is never told, so a fleet user importing a driver-per-row history discovers only later that the column they care about is gone. **This is NOT hard rule 8** (nothing is deleted - the source file is untouched and the rows the app does keep are complete), which is exactly why it needs its own rule: it is a **completeness promise**, and an import that quietly narrows the data is a migration a user cannot trust | **Say what is not coming in, BEFORE the commit, per format.** The review gate is where it belongs (the user has not written anything yet, F6a): name the unsupported columns and how many rows carried a value in them - `"Driver, payment method and discount are not imported (250 rows carry a driver)"` - so the number tells the user whether it matters to them. **Make it data, not code**: each `format` in `GET /import/formats` declares its unsupported fields, so a new importer cannot forget to and the copy is not hardcoded per parser. **Say it once, calmly** - this is a notice at the gate, never a blocking dialog and never an error (hard rule 7: it names what to do instead, which is 'the file stays on your phone if you need those columns'). **Decide** whether an unsupported column with **zero** non-empty rows is mentioned at all; my preference is no - a notice about nothing is noise | L1: the parse result declares the unsupported columns for its format, with the count of rows carrying a value in each. L1: a column empty in every row is not reported (or is, if the row decides otherwise - assert the decision). L4: the review gate shows the notice with the counts, and Continue is never blocked by it. **Vacuous traps**: hardcoding the copy in the client per format, which is the thing that rots; listing every unsupported column including the empty ones, which buries the one that matters; asserting the notice exists without asserting the COUNT, since the count is what makes it actionable; blocking the import on it |
| **[x]** RV.113 | **Import a Drivvo export (positioning: Drivvo is the fleet-shaped incumbent).** Product owner, 2026-09-07, with a real export (`20260907_125214_DRIVVO.csv`, 60 KB). **Measured, not assumed** - an anonymised copy sits at `Spike/ImportFixtures/drivvo/` and is **deliberately gitignored until the owner validates it** (driver names are SHA-256 hashed to `driver-<12 hex>`; the raw file names a third party). The shape is nothing like MFM's: **one file, three sections** - `##Refuelling` (250 rows, 29 columns), `##Expense` (11 rows, 11 cols), `##Service` (54 rows, 10 cols) - each with its own header line, so `MfmParser`'s "title line names the kind" model does not transfer and the parser must scan for section markers. **Four findings that decide the work**: (1) **the headers are LOCALISED** - this export is Russian (`"Одометр (км)"`, `"Дата"`, `"Топливо"`), so a header-name parser breaks for every other UI language; (2) **there is NO currency column anywhere** - MFM had one, and hard rule 3 makes money a pair, so the currency must be established rather than guessed; (3) **there is no vehicle column** - a Drivvo export is one car per file, so [RV.93]'s multi-car mapping does not apply; (4) **mixed decimal conventions inside one row** - `"37.52"` volume with a dot beside `"6,414 л/100км"` consumption with a comma. Also present: `"Полный бак"` as localised Да/Нет, second/third fuel columns for bi-fuel, EV columns (charge type, start/end %, duration) in the same refuelling section, and a `Водитель` column - the fleet feature that is Drivvo's positioning | **Decided by the product owner, 2026-09-07**: **expect exports to be multi-language and validate RU and EN to be safe** - build the header mapping so a second language is data, not a code change, and prove it with both. **The currency is ASKED, not guessed**: the wizard offers the destination car's home currency as the default and lets the user choose another, because a Drivvo file cannot say (hard rule 13 - a default input, never a fact). **This applies to every importer without a currency column, not only Drivvo** - write the rule down where import mappings live. **Keep the server contract narrow**: this is one more `format` for the existing `POST /import/parse`, which stays a pure function that commits nothing (hard rule 9's named exception; a second endpoint is NOT licensed). **Do not** ship the fixture until the owner has validated the anonymisation - the `.gitignore` line and this row are removed in the same change that commits it | L1 backend: the three sections parse into their own candidate kinds from one file; a RU header set and an EN header set both map to the same fields; a row with a comma decimal and a row with a dot decimal both parse; `"0.0"` odometer maps to null, not a zero reading. L1: an export with no currency column produces candidates whose currency comes from the user's answer, and NEVER a hardcoded default. L4: the wizard asks the currency question once and the answer reaches every committed row. **Vacuous traps**: parsing only the RU headers, which is the file in hand and the reason this row names EN too; testing one section and calling the file supported; defaulting the currency silently to the car's and never asking, which passes every test and is the decision this row exists to make; asserting a row count rather than what a row became |
| **[ ]** RV.114 | **Six pump photos and two pump/receipt pairs from a real Estonian forecourt, unscored.** Added 2026-09-07 from the product owner's own capture session (EXIF stripped, HEIC transcoded to JPEG): `pump-067`..`pump-072` with ground truth in `fixtures/pump/expected.csv`, and `receipt-049`..`receipt-051` in `fixtures/receipts/expected.csv`. **What they add that the corpus did not have**: **two matched pump-and-receipt pairs** for the same transaction - `pump-067` + `receipt-049` (Pump 4, 48.37 L, 99.59 EUR) and `pump-068` + `receipt-050` (Pump 7, 70.70 L, 148.40 EUR) - so a cross-check can be scored against a second source of truth rather than a hand-typed expectation; **a 90-degree-rotated pump** (`pump-069`), **a 90-degree-rotated receipt** (`receipt-050`) and an **UPSIDE-DOWN (180-degree) receipt** (`receipt-051`, a Gazpromneft G-Drive 95 fuel-card slip in Cyrillic) - three orientations the corpus had no example of, and 180 degrees is the one a human reader barely notices while OCR fails completely; **a mid-segment digit** (`pump-071`, the last litre digit caught between numerals); **heavy glare obscuring the total while the volume stays crisp** (`pump-067`, whose paired receipt gives the answer); and **a four-grade price board whose displayed prices do NOT include the transaction's** (`pump-072`: 10.01 L for 18.96 EUR is 1.894/L, while the board reads 2.099/2.109/2.119/2.059 - a loyalty discount), which is the shape that makes "read the price off the board" wrong | **Score them and let the numbers move the gate, not the other way round.** Run the harness (`swift run ReceiptSpike <folder>`), record what the parser gets right and wrong per image, and **update `HIGH-WATER.md` and the ratchet honestly** - hard rule 15's measured sentence is re-measured, never assumed. **Decide what a rotated capture should DO** before changing any parser: the app can rotate before OCR, ask the user, or accept the miss - say which, because Vision's own orientation handling is the thing being tested here. **The two pairs are the valuable part**: use them to check that a pump read and its receipt read agree, which is the only place in the corpus where the same fill has two independent sources | L5: the corpus gate runs over the new images and the recorded scores match what the harness actually produced. L1: for each pair, the pump-derived and receipt-derived triples agree to the cent. **Vacuous traps**: adding the images without scoring them, which grows the corpus and proves nothing; lowering the ratchet to accommodate a new miss instead of recording the miss; asserting the files exist |

A user who imported their history specifically to see it cannot. **This is not data loss** (hard rule 8 is intact) - it is a screen that stops where the data does not | **Give the Log a way past 20, and decide which shape before building.** Three coherent answers, and the row must pick ONE and say why: (a) **load more** - the preview grows in pages as the user scrolls, the simplest change and the one that needs no new screen; (b) **a full-log screen** the preview links to, which is what the comment and `docs/SCREENMAP.md` imply was intended - it needs an artboard or an explicit "built from the Home vocabulary" note (`RV.86`'s precedent); (c) **filter by period** - year, or a date range - which the owner explicitly asked for and which is the only one that answers "what did this car cost me in 2024" without scrolling a decade. (a) and (c) compose; (b) is a bigger change. **Whatever is chosen, it must stay local and derived**: no query endpoint (hard rule 9 - there are no domain queries server-side, ever), and the month totals the dividers already draw must keep agreeing with the rows under them. **Watch the cost**: `LogStream` builds sections over `allRows` and Home rebuilds it on every load - a 500-row car is fine, a 5000-row one may not be, so **measure the build time on the owner's real volume** and say what it is rather than assuming | L1 over the paging/filtering: the page after the first never drops or duplicates a row, a purchase group is **never split across a page boundary** (`previewRows` already protects that at the cut - the same must hold per page), and a period filter's month totals equal the sum of the rows it shows. L4 `HomeUITests`: a car with more than 20 entries offers the affordance, and using it reveals a row that was not on screen before. L2/perf: the stream build over the owner's 513-row import, timed and reported. **Vacuous traps**: raising `previewLimit` to a bigger constant and calling it fixed - the next history is longer, and this row is about the missing DOOR, not the number behind it; asserting the affordance exists without asserting a previously hidden row becomes visible; testing with 20 or fewer entries, where nothing is hidden and every assertion passes on today's code; asserting a row count rather than which rows - a page that silently drops an entry counts the same as one that does not |

| **[x]** RV.97 | **Every sync push after a large import dies at the CLIENT'S OWN 30-second timeout, and the cycle relives it forever.** Production log, 2026-09-06 20:05-20:07 (device `787c4f6f`, build `1.0.0+788`): five consecutive `POST /v1/sync/push` requests of **145-151 KB** end **499** (client gone) after **31.8 s, 31.1 s, 13.5 s, 31.9 s and 4.1 s**, while every `GET /v1/sync/pull` in the same minute succeeds in **490-750 ms**. **The 30 s clustering is the tell, and the code names it**: `TransportTimeouts.readJSON = 30` is the idle/read budget for "ordinary JSON calls (sign-in, **sync**, rates, gateway, catalog, config)", while `upload = 120` exists because "blob PUT and import multipart carry megabytes over a mobile uplink". **Sync push carries megabytes too after an import and never asks for the upload budget** - `RemoteSyncTransport` sets no per-request `timeoutInterval`, so a 150 KB body on a mobile uplink runs out of read budget mid-flight. **The server does the work anyway** (31 s of it) and throws the answer away, which is what `499` means. **And the cycle cannot escape**: the batch is `batchLimit: Int = 200` **records**, never bytes, so the same oversized batch is rebuilt and retried every cycle - the pull SCN marches 283 -> 322 -> 372 -> 422 -> 442 -> 492 while the dirty queue never drains. A user who imports their history has a device that pushes 150 KB, fails, and repeats - on their battery and their data. **This is the shape [RV.65] warned about from the other side** (a doubled 53 KB upload visible only because the server logged it) | **Two independent defects; fix both and say which fixed what.** (1) **The budget**: sync push must ask for the upload budget the way blob PUT and import already do - a per-request `timeoutInterval`, not a new global default, because raising `readJSON` would slow every failing GET. (2) **The batch**: `batchLimit` counts RECORDS, so 200 attachments-worth of payload is unbounded in bytes - **bound the batch by encoded size** as well as by count, and say what the limit is and why. **The loop is now MEASURED, not hypothesised** (second log, 20:05-20:22, seventeen minutes): the write is `INSERT`-or-`UPDATE` keyed on `(account_id, id)` (`SyncRepository.cs:51-65`), so a retry does **not** duplicate rows - but it **assigns a new SCN every time**, and that closes the cycle. Push (31 s of real server work) -> client abandons at its 30 s budget and never learns the outcome -> **rows stay dirty locally** -> pull returns the device its OWN just-written rows, fifty at a time (`SinceScn` 492 -> 546 -> 596 on a single-device account, where nothing else can be writing) -> still dirty -> the same 150 KB goes again. Every retry also rewrites the whole account and would wake any OTHER device to re-download everything, forever. Either way the answer belongs in `docs/SYNC.md`. **Do not** simply raise the timeout and leave the batch unbounded - that trades a 30 s failure for a 120 s one on a bigger import | L1: a push batch whose encoded size exceeds the cap splits into several requests, each under it, and every record still lands exactly once. L1: the sync push request carries the upload timeout, not the JSON one (assert the request, not the constant). L2: a push of the owner's import volume completes against the real endpoint. **Vacuous traps**: testing with a handful of small records, where neither limit binds; asserting `batchLimit` changed rather than the encoded SIZE of what goes on the wire; raising the timeout and calling it fixed without a size bound - the next import is bigger |

| **[x]** RV.107 | **The backend PR job runs `dotnet test` and never looks at what ran.** `.github/workflows/backend.yml:65` runs `dotnet test Tankbook.slnx --configuration Release --no-build` with **no TRX inspection at all**, so a pull request is green when Testcontainers fails to start PostgreSQL and the database half of the suite - **210 of 386 declarations** (`docs/BACKEND-TEST-REVIEW.md`) - silently skips. Put to the product owner with two alternatives (atomic-with-metering, which would send a paid answer out unmetered on a failed insert; and best-effort-drop, which loses the record the ledger exists for); **accepted 2026-09-04**, and rule 9 amended in the product owner's voice. | **[x]** RV.50 (2026-09-04, verified by orchestrator: dotnet build 0, format 0, **373 passed (was 368)**, the 5 new tests green; mutation-checked personally - reverting only the enqueue predicate fails 2 of 11 backfill tests, restored byte-identical. **The agent reproduced the bug before fixing it, as required, and found a sharper cause than the brief guessed**: ECB structurally cannot fill a past date (its daily XML carries only today), so a past date with cis/nbk rows but no ecb row is enqueued forever while the backfill correctly finds nothing to fetch. Commit `00db099`.) | **The rate backfill queue never drains: 50 dates processed every 5 minutes, forever, accomplishing nothing.** From production logs 2026-09-04, and it is the clearest signal in them - **20 consecutive passes** from 06:21 to 07:56 reading `rates.backfill Processed=50 Published=0 CarriedForward=0 SourcesFailed=0`. Fifty is exactly `BackfillBatchSize`, so a full batch is drained every pass and **nothing is published, nothing carried, nothing even fails** - and it continues across long windows with no device traffic at all (06:56-07:31 has no sync, no pack, nothing). **`SettleAsync` is called unconditionally** on every date in the batch (`RateBackfillService.cs:101-104`) and deletes by `(base, date)`, so rows ARE being removed - which means they are being **put back**. **Prime suspect, verified as a predicate mismatch in the code**: `RecordRequestAsync` enqueues a date when `!sources.IsSubsetOf(families)` - i.e. when **any** feed family lacks a row - while `BackfillDatesAsync` skips a feed when `families.Contains(feed.Source)` - i.e. it only fetches from feeds that have **no** row. A date that one feed covers and another structurally never will (a feed with no data for that day, a quote outside its remit) therefore satisfies "enqueue me" forever while the backfill correctly concludes there is nothing to fetch. Enqueue -> no-op -> settle -> re-enqueue, permanently. `SourcesFailed=0` says no upstream call is even being made, which fits: the work is skipped, not failed. **Cost today is DB churn rather than money** - no upstream requests are burned - but it is a permanently hot loop, it makes the `rates.backfill` line useless as a health signal (it looks identical whether the system is healthy or wedged), and the next person to read it will assume the backfill is working | **The two predicates must agree on granularity.** Decide whether "covered" means *every feed family has a row* or *this date has a usable rate*, and apply the SAME test on both sides - or a date that is legitimately unfillable will always look pending to one and finished to the other. **A date that cannot be filled must be recorded as ANSWERED, not merely settled**, so it is never re-enqueued: RV.32's own row already named this (*"a date answered 'nothing published' must be recorded as answered, distinct from 'not yet asked'"*) and the carry-back rule was thought to have dissolved it - it did not, for dates outside the 14-day window. **Confirm the mechanism with one query before building**, exactly as RV.30/RV.36 were settled: `SELECT base, count(*), min(date), max(date) FROM rate_backfill GROUP BY base` on two passes five minutes apart - if the same rows persist, it is re-enqueue; if the ids churn, it is something else and this diagnosis is wrong. L2: a date no feed can fill is processed **once** and does not reappear in the next pass - assert the queue is EMPTY on the second pass, not that the first pass succeeded. Mutation-check that assertion |

The 3 s budget was never the mistake; the mistake was assuming the late answer was rare. **Why the upload is not the lever:** `/extract` does not send the full photo - `GatewayRendition` already downscales to an **1800 px long edge at quality 0.9**, and that number was chosen BY the corpus gate because 1600 px scored worse. What is wrong is the assumption underneath: the product was designed as *"the cloud usually answers while you are still on the sheet, and occasionally arrives late"*, and the real distribution is the reverse. ## Dispatch ledger - which model did which task
**Reconstructed 2026-09-05 from the `opencode` transcripts in `/tmp/agentlogs/*.log`**, where each run
records its own model. This is the ground truth behind the flash-vs-pro evaluation, and it is here so
a row's outcome can always be read against the worker that produced it.

**Totals: 24 pro, 20 flash** across 44 build dispatches. Read it with the selection bias in mind -
pro was chosen for tasks *believed* harder, so a raw success comparison carries no information; what
the evaluation compared was failure KINDS and above-brief judgement. `flash*` marks the dispatch that
died instantly with `database is locked` and never reached the model (re-dispatched on flash).

| Task | Worker | Date | Log |
|---|---|---|---|
| `RV.10` | **flash** | 2026-09-03 | 436 KB |
| `RV.14` | **pro** | 2026-09-03 | 415 KB |
| `RV.17` | **pro** | 2026-09-03 | 514 KB |
| `RV.18` | **pro** | 2026-09-03 | 293 KB |
| `RV.21` | **flash** | 2026-09-03 | 464 KB |
| `RV.22` | **pro** | 2026-09-03 | 573 KB |
| `RV.23-VALIDATE` | **pro** | 2026-09-03 | 135 KB |
| `RV.24` | **pro** | 2026-09-03 | 436 KB |

## OB · Observability **[v1.0.x]** - the device can say what it did

**Defined 2026-09-05 (product owner).** Not a new backlog: this groups four rows that already
existed as `PR.9`, `PR.10`, `PR.11` and `PR.12` and names why they belong together. **The evidence
for the grouping is this week's bug hunt.** RV.58, RV.59, RV.60, RV.65 and RV.66 were all diagnosed
from **server** logs, because the device emits almost nothing - and two of them needed the
orchestrator to read client source to guess what the app had done. One of those guesses (RV.58's
cross-account claim) was **wrong and had to be retracted**, precisely because no client-side record
existed to check it against.

The cluster's value is not a feature. It is that the next production report should be answerable
from the device's own account of itself, instead of inferred from what the server happened to see.
**SH.3, the human floor walk, will generate exactly that kind of question**, which is the argument
for landing at least `OB.2` before it.

Build order, and it is a real dependency chain, not a preference:

| Order | Row | Was | Why here |
|---|---|---|---|
| **OB.1** | Error codes on the wire | `PR.9` | A stable `code` is what every later layer maps from; a status alone cannot name a next step (hard rule 7). RV.65's new sign-in message is the first consumer. |
| **OB.2** | Emit the defined events and async edges | `PR.10` | The foundation - nothing can be exported or persisted that was never emitted. Highest value alone: `NetRequest/Response` would have shown RV.59's duplicate config fetch and RV.65's doubled 53 KB upload from the client side. |
| **OB.3** | Persist last sync date and last failure | `PR.12` | Needs OB.1's `code` to record a failure meaningfully. The natural sibling of RV.58's revoked-device card - the state survives a relaunch instead of vanishing. |
| **OB.4** | Diagnostics export end to end | `PR.11` | Needs OB.2's events to have anything worth exporting; it is the user-facing delivery of the other three. |

The four rows keep their `PR.*` ids and their own text - **this section is the ordering and the
rationale, not a duplicate backlog.** Marking any of them done marks the OB step done.

## AG · Car Agent **[v2]** (Pro)

*Mirror of `docs/AGENT.md` §11, which carries the reasoning; this table carries the checks. A
v2 phase behind the Pro tier - nothing here is in the launch triage. Artboards in
`design/screens/v2/`, canvas page "Agent (v2, Pro)". Journeys J14-J17, F11-F12.*

| ID | Task | Checks (done when...) |
|---|---|---|
| **[ ]** AG.1 | **Decisions written down**: the rule-9 second exception (in `CLAUDE.md`), the privacy-policy sentence in `SECURITY.md` and on the site, the Pro entitlement name and per-month turn quota as `llmQuota.agentTurns` in `CONFIG.md`, the five-slot tab bar in `DESIGN.md` (done) | Docs reconciled; product owner signs the quota and the policy sentence |
| **[ ]** AG.2 | **Tab bar: fifth slot** - `AppTabBar` gains Ask (glyph + label, never raised), `Route.ask`, a tab root with its own `NavigationStack`; RU label verified at Dynamic Type XL on 375pt and 390pt, `Чат` wired as fallback if `Спросить` clips | L4 `TankbookShellUITests`: five tabs hittable, capture circle geometry unchanged; XL screenshots EN+RU, dark + light |
| **[ ]** AG.3 | **Ask screen: not-Pro and offline states** (`AgentAsk.dc.html`): examples as taps to Trends / Reminders / Garage, the Pro card -> Paywall, offline / quota / gateway copy from `ERRORS.md` -> Ask; nothing gated | L4 new `AskUITests`: each example opens its screen; a free seed shows the Pro card; `-forceOffline` shows the offline line and every tab still works; EN+RU |
| **[ ]** AG.4 | **`AgentSession` + read tools** in `TankbookCore/Agent/`: `carProfile`, `spend`, `consumption`, `entries`, `lastService`, `reminders`, `anomaly`, `tireSets`, `stations` - pure over the repository, each returning a typed card model | L1 per tool on seeded repositories: figures equal `HomeStats` / `TrendsStats` / `AnomalyEngine` for the same inputs; a tool that computes its own number instead of calling the engine fails |
| **[ ]** AG.5 | **`POST /agent/turn`** on the gateway: locale system frame, provider call, per-account metering in `llm_usage`, 402/429 with `Retry-After`, shape-only logging, stores nothing; `API.md` section | L2: turn round-trip on a recorded provider; quota exhaustion -> 429; the log sweep finds no prompt or answer text; `RedactionTests` extended with a turn body |
| **[ ]** AG.6 | **Thread UI** (`AgentChat.dc.html`): user turns, tool-result cards rendered by the app (`StatTile`, entry rows, station table), narration beneath, "Open in Trends" and entry deep links, composer with dictation and camera; conversation persisted on device per car, never synced | L4 `AskUITests` over a recorded transport: the J14 fixture renders its cards with the app's figures; **mutation: a number in narration absent from the cards fails** (`AGENT.md` §8); EN+RU |
| **[ ]** AG.7 | **Draft tools + confirm hand-off**: `draftReminder` -> `ReminderFormView`, `draftServiceEntry` -> `ServiceEntryView`, `draftFillUp` -> `ManualFillUpView`, `draftExpense`, `draftNote`; fields dimmed until touched; saved / cancelled returned to the session; `provenance = .agent` | L1: no draft writes without the screen's Save. L4 `AskUITests` + `RemindersUITests`: the J15 RU fixture opens the form with exactly its fields, Save persists, swipe-down persists nothing |
| **[ ]** AG.8 | **Consent and privacy surface**: first-turn consent sheet listing exactly what is sent, the car context card visible in the thread, "That's not right" with opt-in thread attach, diagnostics export carrying turn shapes only | L4: consent shown once per install, never again. L1: attach flag off by default; `LoggingTests` sweep over a turn |
| **[ ]** AG.9 | **Invoice through the agent** (`AgentInvoice.dc.html`): `captureInvoice()` via the document camera, items card with per-line explanation and category, unplaced lines marked, "Save as service entry" -> `ServiceEntryView`, shelf-part suggestion, lifetime follow-up -> AG.7 | L1: placed + unplaced sums equal the invoice total. L4 `AskUITests` with a fixture invoice; EN+RU |
| **[ ]** AG.10 | **Diagnosis** (`AgentDiagnosis.dc.html`): context card first, ranked causes each with an evidence label, the fixed urgency row (amber "book this week", system-red dialog only for "stop driving"), safety-critical escalation in the system frame, the three app-action buttons, the once-only second-opinion line | L1: every cause carries a label; brake / steering / fuel-smell fixtures never resolve to "drive on". L4: the row and the dialog; EN+RU |
| **[ ]** AG.11 | **The gate** (`AGENT.md` §8): fixtures under `ios/Tests/Fixtures/agent/`, an L1 tool-routing suite in CI on a recorded model, an L5 answer-quality run on demand with its ratchet in `TESTING.md` | **Mutation: let the model state a figure directly -> the gate fails.** Ratchet recorded |
| **[ ]** AG.12 | **Paywall + entitlement** (the P6.16-deferred Pro tier, now with its reason): StoreKit subscription / lifetime, entitlement check on the Ask tab and `/extract`, the free tier's "everything else stays free - including export" line | L2 receipt validation. L4: Paywall reachable only from the Ask card and the car-limit sheet |
| **[ ]** AG.13 | **Re-check on-device models** at each iOS release: a Russian-capable model on the floor hardware routes `AgentSession` locally and drops the account requirement for that path | A recorded decision per release in `VISION.md` -> "Why tier 2 was cut" |
| **[ ]** AG.14 | **Ground the answer in what a general model cannot have, and say so on the screen.** From the 2026-09-06 market read (`diagnostics/RESEARCH-agent-market.md`): no established fuel log ships conversational AI over the owner's records - Drivvo, Fuelio, Fuelly, aCar, Simply Auto, CarScope and Spritmonitor all lack it - but users already troubleshoot with free ChatGPT and Gemini and **say so in reviews**, so an undifferentiated car chatbot reads as repackaged search. The seven things buyers named as worth paying for are: their own fuel/expense/service history, VIN and manual grounding, live OBD data, guided tests with confidence, exact parts and diagrams, writing back to the log, and a safety escalation. **Tankbook owns two of them outright (history, write-back) and deliberately owns none of the diagnostic ones.** The tool catalogue (`AGENT.md` §4) is already the first; the draft tools are already the sixth | Every answer that used the user's data **shows which rows it read** - the card is the evidence, not decoration (`AGENT.md` numbers-from-tools rule). L4: an answer about spend opens the entries behind it. **Vacuous trap**: a citation line that names a tool without opening what it returned |
| **[ ]** AG.15 | **Price and metering, informed by what the market actually charges.** Measured 2026-09-06: the apps doing exactly this - Refuells **$1.99/mo or $19.99/yr**, Brimly **$2.99/mo** with 10 free questions a day, GarageHub **$7.99/mo** with ~15 free messages, MECH AI **$7.99/mo** with 3-5 a day, MyAutoLog **$3.99/mo** - all sit at **$2-8/month with a metered free tier**, while the $100/year products (FIXD, Carly) bundle hardware or human mechanics. **The review evidence is unambiguous on two failure modes** and both are ours to avoid: FIXD's worst reviews are about auto-renewal and cancellation rather than answer quality, and a paying MECH AI user found "unlimited" showing zero messages - **an entitlement bug erases willingness to pay faster than a bad answer does**. Feeds AG.12's paywall and `CONFIG.md`'s `llmQuota.agentTurns` | The quota the user sees equals the quota enforced, proven by a test that exhausts it and reads the number back. Cancellation is reachable from the paywall in one tap and says what happens to the log (nothing). **Vacuous traps**: asserting the paywall renders a price; testing the quota only at zero |
| **[ ]** AG.16 | **The three answers the market says people actually ask, as the examples on an empty Ask tab.** Not invented: they are what the shipped competitors demonstrate (`diagnostics/RESEARCH-agent-market.md`) - Refuells answers "what did I spend last month", "how has efficiency changed", "which stations do I use"; GarageHub's Rev answers "what is overdue" and writes the log entry. Ours already have tools behind them (`spend`, `consumption`, `anomaly`, `reminders`, `stations`). **The empty state is the pitch**: a free user who never asks a question never learns what the tab is for, and `AGENT.md` §3 already says the examples are taps to the ordinary screens for a non-Pro user | L4 `AskUITests`: each example produces an answer whose card opens the screen the data lives on; for a non-Pro user each example opens that screen directly. **Vacuous trap**: asserting three buttons exist rather than what each produces |
| **[ ]** AG.17 | **A safety stance the reviews prove is needed, written before the first answer ships.** Recorded 2026-09-06 from the market read: an AI mechanic "inferred the wrong cause from a fault code" and a reviewer said it "could have caused expensive unnecessary repairs"; mechanics on r/MechanicAdvice reported generative AI doubling down on unsafe brake advice. `AGENT.md` §5's diagnosis framing and the answer gate exist for this, and this row is where they get their teeth: **braking, steering, fuel and high-voltage answers escalate to a professional and never end in a confident single cause**, uncertainty is stated rather than implied, and no answer recommends replacing a part from a code alone. **This is also the honest limit of our position**: we have no live diagnostic data, so diagnosis here is reasoning over history and symptoms, and the copy must not imply otherwise | L1: a symptom in a safety-critical category always produces the escalation line, whatever the model returned. L1: an answer with no supporting rows says so instead of asserting. **Vacuous traps**: testing the happy path only; asserting the disclaimer's presence rather than that it fires on the category |

## Working rules

- One task = one PR = code + its checks; the PR description quotes the task ID and its check list, ticked.
- A task discovering an undocumented behavior extends the owning doc in the same PR (CLAUDE.md rule).
- Snapshot baselines are reviewed like code; accuracy high-water marks only ratchet up.
